</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Julian of Norwich</b> (1343<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup>&#160;– after 1416), also known as <b>Juliana of Norwich</b>, <b>the Lady Julian</b>, <b>Dame Julian</b> or <b>Mother Julian</b>, was an <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_England" title="Kingdom of England">English</a> <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Anchorite" title="Anchorite">anchoress</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>. Her writings, now known as <i><a href="/wiki/Revelations_of_Divine_Love" title="Revelations of Divine Love">Revelations of Divine Love</a></i>, are the earliest surviving English language works by a woman, although it is possible that some anonymous works may have had female authors. They are also the only surviving English language works by an anchoress.
</p><p>Julian lived in the English city of <a href="/wiki/Norwich#Middle_Ages" title="Norwich">Norwich</a>, an important centre for commerce that also had a vibrant religious life. During her lifetime, the city suffered the devastating effects of the <a href="/wiki/Black_Death_in_England" title="Black Death in England">Black Death</a> of 1348–1350, the <a href="/wiki/Peasants%27_Revolt" title="Peasants&#39; Revolt">Peasants' Revolt</a> (which affected large parts of England in 1381), and the suppression of the <a href="/wiki/Lollardy" title="Lollardy">Lollards</a>. In 1373, aged 30 and so seriously ill she thought she was on her deathbed, Julian received a series of <a href="/wiki/Vision_(spirituality)" title="Vision (spirituality)">visions</a> or <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special:Search/shew" class="extiw" title="wikt:Special:Search/shew">shewings</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Passion_(Christianity)" class="mw-redirect" title="Passion (Christianity)">Passion of Christ</a>. She recovered from her illness and wrote two versions of her experiences, the earlier one being completed soon after her recovery—a much longer version, today known as the <i>Long Text</i>, was written many years later.
</p><p>Julian lived in permanent <a href="/wiki/Seclusion" title="Seclusion">seclusion</a> as an anchoress in her <a href="/wiki/Monastic_cell" title="Monastic cell">cell</a>, which was attached to <a href="/wiki/St_Julian%27s_Church,_Norwich" title="St Julian&#39;s Church, Norwich">St Julian's Church, Norwich</a>. Four <a href="/wiki/Will_and_testament" title="Will and testament">wills</a> are known in which sums were bequeathed to a Norwich anchoress named Julian, and an account by the celebrated mystic <a href="/wiki/Margery_Kempe" title="Margery Kempe">Margery Kempe</a> exists which provides evidence of counsel Kempe was given by the anchoress.
</p><p>Details of Julian's family, education, or of her life before becoming an anchoress are not known; it is unclear whether her actual name was Julian. Preferring to write anonymously, and seeking isolation from the world, she was nevertheless influential in her lifetime. While her writings were carefully preserved, the <a href="/wiki/Reformation" title="Reformation">Reformation</a> prevented their publication in print. The <i>Long Text</i> was first published in 1670 by the <a href="/wiki/Benedictines" title="Benedictines">Benedictine</a> monk <a href="/wiki/Serenus_de_Cressy" title="Serenus de Cressy">Serenus de Cressy</a>, reissued by George Hargreaves Parker in 1843, and published in a modernised version in 1864. Julian's writings emerged from obscurity in 1901 when a manuscript in the <a href="/wiki/British_Museum" title="British Museum">British Museum</a> was transcribed and published with notes by <a href="/wiki/Grace_Harriet_Warrack" class="mw-redirect" title="Grace Harriet Warrack">Grace Warrack</a>; many translations have been made since. Julian is today considered to be an important Christian mystic and <a href="/wiki/Theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Theologian">theologian</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Background">Background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:332px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_Norwich_(c.1300)_by_Woodward.jpg" class="image"><img alt="map of 14th century Norwich" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Map_of_Norwich_%28c.1300%29_by_Woodward.jpg/330px-Map_of_Norwich_%28c.1300%29_by_Woodward.jpg" decoding="async" width="330" height="408" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Map_of_Norwich_%28c.1300%29_by_Woodward.jpg/495px-Map_of_Norwich_%28c.1300%29_by_Woodward.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Map_of_Norwich_%28c.1300%29_by_Woodward.jpg/660px-Map_of_Norwich_%28c.1300%29_by_Woodward.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2278" data-file-height="2817" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_Norwich_(c.1300)_by_Woodward.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of <a href="/wiki/Norwich" title="Norwich">Norwich</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1300</span>) by <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Woodward" title="Samuel Woodward">Samuel Woodward</a> (1847); <a href="/wiki/St_Julian%27s_Church,_Norwich" title="St Julian&#39;s Church, Norwich">St Julian's Church</a>, towards the bottom of the map, is labelled with a '7'.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_England" title="Kingdom of England">English</a> city of <a href="/wiki/Norwich" title="Norwich">Norwich</a>, where Julian probably lived all her life, was second in importance to <a href="/wiki/Norman_and_Medieval_London" title="Norman and Medieval London">London during the 13th and 14th centuries</a>, and the centre of the country's primary region for agriculture and trade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201617_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201617-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;note 2&#93;</a></sup> During her lifetime the <a href="/wiki/Black_Death" title="Black Death">Black Death</a> reached Norwich; the disease may have killed over half the population of the city, and returned in <a href="/wiki/Second_plague_pandemic#Recurrence_throughout_Europe" title="Second plague pandemic">subsequent outbreaks</a> up to 1387.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201624_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201624-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Julian was alive during the <a href="/wiki/Peasants%27_Revolt" title="Peasants&#39; Revolt">Peasants' Revolt</a> of 1381, when the city was overwhelmed by rebel forces led by Geoffrey Litster. <a href="/wiki/Henry_le_Despenser" title="Henry le Despenser">Henry le Despenser</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Norwich" title="Bishop of Norwich">Bishop of Norwich</a>, executed Litster after the peasant army was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_North_Walsham" title="Battle of North Walsham">Battle of North Walsham</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201625–26_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201625–26-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Despenser zealously opposed the <a href="/wiki/Lollardy" title="Lollardy">Lollards</a>, who advocated reform of the Church, and some of them were <a href="/wiki/Death_by_burning#Medieval_Inquisition_and_the_burning_of_heretics" title="Death by burning">burnt at the stake</a> at <a href="/wiki/Lollards_Pit" title="Lollards Pit">Lollards Pit</a>, just outside the city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201617_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201617-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Norwich may have been one of the most religious cities in Europe at that time, with <a href="/wiki/Norwich_Cathedral" title="Norwich Cathedral">its cathedral</a>, <a href="/wiki/Priory" title="Priory">friaries</a>, churches and recluses' cells dominating both the landscape and the lives of its citizens. On the eastern side of the city was the cathedral priory (founded in 1096), the Benedictine <a href="/wiki/History_of_hospitals#Medieval_European_hospitals" title="History of hospitals">Hospital</a> of St Paul, the <a href="/wiki/Carmelite" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmelite">Carmelite</a> friary, <a href="/wiki/Great_Hospital" title="Great Hospital">St Giles's Hospital</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Greyfriars</a> monastery. To the south, the <a href="/wiki/Carrow_Abbey" title="Carrow Abbey">priory at Carrow</a> was located just beyond the city walls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERawcliffeWilson200488_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERawcliffeWilson200488-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Its income was mainly generated from "<a href="/wiki/Benefice" title="Benefice">livings</a>" acquired from the renting of its assets, which included the Norwich churches of <a href="/wiki/St_Julian%27s_Church,_Norwich" title="St Julian&#39;s Church, Norwich">St Julian</a>, All Saints Timberhill, St Edward Conisford and St Catherine Newgate, all now lost apart from St Julian's. The churches with <a href="/wiki/Anchorite" title="Anchorite">anchorite</a> cells enhanced the reputation of the priory, as they attracted <a href="/wiki/Financial_endowment" title="Financial endowment">endowments</a> from across society.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERawcliffeWilson200489_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERawcliffeWilson200489-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Sources_for_Julian.27s_life"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources_for_Julian's_life">Sources for Julian's life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Sources for Julian&#039;s life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Little of Julian's life is known. She provided a few scant comments about herself in her own writings,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> later published in a book commonly known as <i><a href="/wiki/Revelations_of_Divine_Love" title="Revelations of Divine Love">Revelations of Divine Love</a></i>, a title first used in 1670.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015lii_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015lii-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The earliest surviving copy of a manuscript of Julian's, made by a <a href="/wiki/Scribe" title="Scribe">scribe</a> in the 1470s, acknowledges her as the author of the work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The earliest known references to Julian come from four <a href="/wiki/Will_and_testament" title="Will and testament">wills</a>, in which she is described as being an <a href="/wiki/Anchoress" class="mw-redirect" title="Anchoress">anchoress</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The wills were all made by individuals who lived in <a href="/wiki/Norfolk" title="Norfolk">Norfolk</a>. Roger Reed, the rector of <a href="/wiki/St_Michael_Coslany,_Norwich" title="St Michael Coslany, Norwich">St Michael Coslany, Norwich</a>, whose will of 20<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>March 1394 provides the earliest record of Julian's existence, made a <a href="/wiki/Bequest" class="mw-redirect" title="Bequest">bequest</a> of 12<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Shilling#Kingdom_of_England" title="Shilling">shillings</a> to be paid to "Julian anakorite".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Thomas Edmund, a <a href="/wiki/Chantry" title="Chantry">Chantry</a> priest from <a href="/wiki/Aylsham" title="Aylsham">Aylsham</a>, stipulated in his will of 19<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>May 1404 that 12<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Penny#England" title="Penny">pennies</a> be given to "Julian, anchoress of the church of St Julian, Conisford" and 8 pennies to "Sarah, living with her".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;note 3&#93;</a></sup> John Plumpton from Norwich gave 40 pennies to "the anchoress in the church of St Julian's, Conisford, and a shilling each to her maid and her former maid Alice" in his will dated 24<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>November 1415.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The fourth person to mention Julian was Isabelle, Countess of Suffolk (the second wife of <a href="/wiki/William_de_Ufford,_2nd_Earl_of_Suffolk" class="mw-redirect" title="William de Ufford, 2nd Earl of Suffolk">William de Ufford, 2nd Earl of Suffolk</a>), who made a bequest of 20<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>shillings to "<span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Julian reclus a Norwich</i></span>" in her will dated 26 September 1416.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> As a bequest to an unnamed anchorite at St Julian's was made in 1429, there is a possibility Julian was alive at this time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199411_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199411-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe,_Chapter_18_(excerpt).png" class="image"><img alt="manuscript of page of Kempe&#39;s book" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe%2C_Chapter_18_%28excerpt%29.png/220px-The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe%2C_Chapter_18_%28excerpt%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe%2C_Chapter_18_%28excerpt%29.png/330px-The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe%2C_Chapter_18_%28excerpt%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe%2C_Chapter_18_%28excerpt%29.png/440px-The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe%2C_Chapter_18_%28excerpt%29.png 2x" data-file-width="793" data-file-height="446" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe,_Chapter_18_(excerpt).png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Part of the manuscript (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1440</span>) dictated by the mystic <a href="/wiki/Margery_Kempe" title="Margery Kempe">Margery Kempe</a> to a <a href="/wiki/Scribe" title="Scribe">scribe</a>, in which she mentions visiting "dame jelyan" (<a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a>)</div></div></div>
<p>Julian was known as a <a href="/wiki/Spirituality" title="Spirituality">spiritual authority</a> within her community, where she also served as an adviser.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiii_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiii-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> In around 1414, when she was in her seventies, she was visited by the English <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystic</a> <a href="/wiki/Margery_Kempe" title="Margery Kempe">Margery Kempe</a>. <i><a href="/wiki/The_Book_of_Margery_Kempe" title="The Book of Margery Kempe">The Book of Margery Kempe</a></i>, which is possibly the first autobiography to be written in English,<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> mentions that Kempe travelled to Norwich to obtain spiritual advice from Julian,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015viii_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015viii-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> saying she was "bidden by Our Lord" to go to "Dame Jelyan ... for the anchoress was expert in" divine revelations, "and good counsel could give".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButler-BowdenChambers195454_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButler-BowdenChambers195454-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Kempe never referred to Julian as an author, although she was familiar with the works of other spiritual writers, and mentioned them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199411_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199411-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Visions">Visions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Visions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Julian wrote in <i>Revelations of Divine Love</i> that she became seriously ill at the age of 30. She could have been an anchoress when she fell ill, although it is possible she was a <a href="/wiki/Laity" title="Laity">lay person</a> living at home,<sup id="cite_ref-ABC_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> as she was visited by her mother and other people, and the <a href="/wiki/Enclosed_religious_orders" title="Enclosed religious orders">rules of enclosure</a> for an anchoress would not normally have allowed outsiders such access.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015x,_xv_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015x,_xv-22">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> On 8<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>May 1373 a <a href="/wiki/Curate" title="Curate">curate</a> administered the <a href="/wiki/Last_rites" title="Last rites">last rites</a> of the Church to her, in anticipation of her death. As he held a <a href="/wiki/Crucifix" title="Crucifix">crucifix</a> above the foot of her bed, she began to lose her sight and feel physically numb, but gazing on the crucifix she saw the figure of Jesus begin to bleed. Over the next several hours, she had a series of 15 visions of Jesus, and a 16th the following night.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015ix_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015ix-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201637_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201637-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julian completely recovered from her illness on 13<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>May;<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> there is general agreement that she wrote about her <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special:Search/shew" class="extiw" title="wikt:Special:Search/shew">shewings</a> shortly after she experienced them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002219_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002219-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Her original manuscript no longer exists, but a copy, now known as the <i>Short Text</i>,  survived.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGinn2012425_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGinn2012425-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Decades later, perhaps in the early 1390s, she began a <a href="/wiki/Theological" class="mw-redirect" title="Theological">theological</a> exploration of the meaning of her visions, and produced writings now known as <i>The Long Text</i>. This second work seems to have gone through many revisions before it was finished, perhaps in the 1410s or 1420s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGinn2012425_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGinn2012425-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Julian's <a href="/wiki/Revelation" title="Revelation">revelations</a> seem to be the first important example of a vision by an Englishwoman for 200 years, in contrast with the Continent, where "a golden age of women's mysticism" occurred during the 13th and 14th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002219_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002219-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Revelations_of_Divine_Love_(Add_MS_37790)_f._97r.png" class="image"><img alt="15th century manuscript of Julian&#39;s Short Text" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28Add_MS_37790%29_f._97r.png/220px-Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28Add_MS_37790%29_f._97r.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="81" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28Add_MS_37790%29_f._97r.png/330px-Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28Add_MS_37790%29_f._97r.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28Add_MS_37790%29_f._97r.png/440px-Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28Add_MS_37790%29_f._97r.png 2x" data-file-width="1217" data-file-height="449" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Revelations_of_Divine_Love_(Add_MS_37790)_f._97r.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The beginning of the 15th century <i>Short Text</i>. "<i>Here es a vision schewed be the goodenes of god to a devoute woman and hir name es Julyan that is recluse atte Norwyche and zitt ys on lyfe anno domini millesimo ccccxiii</i>". (<a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">BL</a>, Add MS 37790)</div></div></div>
<p>The few autobiographical details Julian included in the <i>Short Text</i>, including her gender, were suppressed when she wrote her longer text later in life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiii_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiii-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Historians are not even sure of her actual name. It is generally thought to be taken from the church in Norwich to which her cell was attached, but <i>Julian</i> was also used in its own right as a girl's name in the Middle Ages, and so could have been her <a href="/wiki/Christian_name" title="Christian name">Christian name</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGroves201074_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGroves201074-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julian's writings indicate that she was born in 1343 or late 1342, and died after 1416.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer1992130_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer1992130-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> She was six when the Black Death arrived in Norwich.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201813_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201813-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> It has been speculated that she was educated as a young girl by the Benedictine <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nuns</a> of Carrow Abbey, as a school for girls existed there during her childhood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer1992130_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer1992130-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatsonJenkins20064_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatsonJenkins20064-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> There is no written evidence that she was ever a nun at Carrow.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to several commentators, including Santha Bhattacharji in the <i><a href="/wiki/Oxford_Dictionary_of_National_Biography" class="mw-redirect" title="Oxford Dictionary of National Biography">Oxford Dictionary of National Biography</a></i>, Julian's discussion of the maternal nature of God suggests that she knew of motherhood from her own experience of bringing up children.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> As plague epidemics were rampant during the 14th century, it has been suggested that Julian may have lost her own family as a result.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObbard200816_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObbard200816-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> By becoming an anchoress she would have been kept in <a href="/wiki/Quarantine" title="Quarantine">quarantine</a> away from the rest of the population of Norwich.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer1992130_29-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer1992130-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> However, nothing in Julian's writings provides any indication of the plagues, religious conflict, or civil insurrection that occurred in the city during her lifetime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201631_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201631-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_Leech" title="Kenneth Leech">Kenneth Leech</a> and Sister <a href="/wiki/Benedicta_Ward" title="Benedicta Ward">Benedicta Ward</a>, the joint authors of <i>Julian Reconsidered</i> (1988), concluded that she was a young widowed mother and never a nun. They based their opinion on a dearth of references about her occupation in life and a lack of evidence to connect her with Carrow Abbey, which would have honoured her and buried her in the grounds had she been strongly connected with the priory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeechWard199521_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeechWard199521-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Life_as_an_anchoress">Life as an anchoress</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Life as an anchoress">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:CCCC_MS_79_fol72r_300px.jpg" class="image"><img alt="illustration of a medieval bishop blessing an anchoress" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/CCCC_MS_79_fol72r_300px.jpg/220px-CCCC_MS_79_fol72r_300px.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="224" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/5d/CCCC_MS_79_fol72r_300px.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="307" data-file-height="312" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:CCCC_MS_79_fol72r_300px.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A bishop blessing an anchoress, from MS 079: Pontifical (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1400</span>&#160;– c.<span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1410</span>), <a href="/wiki/Corpus_Christi_College,_Cambridge" title="Corpus Christi College, Cambridge">Corpus Christi College, Cambridge</a></div></div></div>
<p>Julian was an anchoress from at least the 1390s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Living in her cell, she would have played an important part within her community, devoting herself to a life of prayer to complement the <a href="/wiki/Clergy" title="Clergy">clergy</a> in their primary function as protectors of souls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201611_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201611-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Her solitary life would have begun after the completion of an onerous selection process.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002206_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002206-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> An important church ceremony would have taken place at St Julian's Church, in the presence of the <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf201850_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf201850-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> During the ceremony, <a href="/wiki/Psalms" title="Psalms">psalms</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Office_of_the_Dead" title="Office of the Dead">Office of the Dead</a> would have been sung for Julian (as if it were her funeral), and at some point she would have been led to her cell door and into the room beyond.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201613_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201613-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The door would afterwards have been sealed up, and she would have remained in her cell for the rest of her life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez20165,_13_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez20165,_13-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Once her life of seclusion had begun, Julian would have had to follow the strict rules laid down for anchoresses. Two important sources of information about the life of such women have survived. <i>De institutione inclusarum</i> was written in <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> by <a href="/wiki/Aelred_of_Rievaulx" title="Aelred of Rievaulx">Ælred of Rieveaulx</a> in around 1162, and the <i><a href="/wiki/Ancrene_Wisse" title="Ancrene Wisse">Ancrene Riwle</a></i> was written in <a href="/wiki/Middle_English" title="Middle English">Middle English</a> in around 1200.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002210,_212_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002210,_212-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFugelso2020127_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFugelso2020127-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;note 4&#93;</a></sup> Originally made for three sisters, the <i>Ancrene Riwle</i> became in time a manual for all female recluses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002211_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002211-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The work regained its former popularity during the mystical movement of the 14th century. It may have been available to Julian to read and become familiar with—being a book written in a language she could read.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker1993149_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1993149-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The book stipulated that anchoresses should live in confined isolation, in <a href="/wiki/Vow_of_poverty" class="mw-redirect" title="Vow of poverty">poverty</a>, and under a <a href="/wiki/Vow_of_chastity" class="mw-redirect" title="Vow of chastity">vow of chastity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201611_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201611-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The popular image of Julian living with her cat for company stems from the regulations set out in the <i><a href="/wiki/Ancrene_Riwle" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancrene Riwle">Ancrene Riwle</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201611–13_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201611–13-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As an anchoress living in the heart of an urban environment, Julian would not have been entirely secluded. She would have enjoyed the financial support of the more prosperous members of the local community, as well as the general affection of the population. She would have in turn provided prayers and given advice to visitors, serving as an example of devout holiness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xii–xiii_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xii–xiii-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> According to one edition of the <i><a href="/wiki/Cambridge_Medieval_History" class="mw-redirect" title="Cambridge Medieval History">Cambridge Medieval History</a></i>, it is possible that she met the English mystic <a href="/wiki/Walter_Hilton" title="Walter Hilton">Walter Hilton</a>, who died when Julian was in her fifties, and who may have influenced her writings in a small way.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETannerPrevité-OrtonBrooke1932807_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETannerPrevité-OrtonBrooke1932807-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Revelations_of_Divine_Love"><i>Revelations of Divine Love</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Revelations of Divine Love">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Revelations_of_Divine_Love" title="Revelations of Divine Love">Revelations of Divine Love</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_(title_page,_1670_edition).jpg" class="image"><img alt="frontispiece of 1670 edition of Revelations of Divine Love" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28title_page%2C_1670_edition%29.jpg/170px-XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28title_page%2C_1670_edition%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="304" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28title_page%2C_1670_edition%29.jpg/255px-XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28title_page%2C_1670_edition%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28title_page%2C_1670_edition%29.jpg/340px-XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_%28title_page%2C_1670_edition%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="543" data-file-height="972" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:XVI_Revelations_of_Divine_Love_(title_page,_1670_edition).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>First edition of <i>XVI Revelations of Divine Love</i> (1670)</div></div></div>
<p>Both the <i>Long Text</i> and <i>Short Text</i> of Julian's <i>Revelations of Divine Love</i> contain an account of each of her revelations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015lii_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015lii-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Her writings are unique, as they are the earliest surviving English language works by a woman, although it is possible that some anonymous works may have had female authors. They are also the only surviving writings by an English anchoress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002208_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002208-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015ix_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015ix-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Long Text</i> consists of 86 chapters and about 63,500 words, and is about six times longer than the <i>Short Text</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJantzen20114–5_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJantzen20114–5-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002219_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002219-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 14th century England, when women were generally barred from high status positions, their knowledge of Latin would have been limited, and it is more likely that they read and wrote in English.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002211_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002211-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The historian <a href="/wiki/Janina_Ramirez" title="Janina Ramirez">Janina Ramirez</a> has suggested that by choosing to write in her <a href="/wiki/Vernacular" title="Vernacular">vernacular</a> language, a precedent set by other medieval writers, Julian was "attempting to express the inexpressible" in the best way possible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez20167_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez20167-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Nothing written by Julian was ever mentioned in any bequests, nor written for a specific readership, or influenced other medieval authors,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf20138_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf20138-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> and almost no references were made to her writings from the time they were written until the beginning of the 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeechWard199512_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeechWard199512-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julian's writings were largely unknown until 1670, when they were published under the title <i>XVI Revelations of Divine Love, shewed to a devout servant of Our Lord, called Mother Juliana, an Anchorete of Norwich: Who lived in the Dayes of King Edward the Third</i> by <a href="/wiki/Serenus_de_Cressy" title="Serenus de Cressy">Serenus de Cressy</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Confession_(religion)" title="Confession (religion)">confessor</a> for the English nuns at <a href="/wiki/Cambrai" title="Cambrai">Cambrai</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199411_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199411-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BL1_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BL1-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Cressy based his book on the <i>Long Text</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201678_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201678-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> probably written by Julian in the 1410s or 1420s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGinn2012425_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGinn2012425-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Three manuscript copies of the <i>Long Text</i> have survived.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xx_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xx-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> One copy of the complete <i>Long Text</i>, known as the Paris Manuscript, resides in the <a href="/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France" title="Bibliothèque nationale de France">Bibliothèque nationale de France</a> in Paris, and two other manuscripts are now in the <a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a> in London.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199420–21_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199420–21-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> One of the manuscripts was perhaps copied out by Dame Clementina Cary, who founded the English Benedictine monastery in Paris.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeechWard199512_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeechWard199512-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Cressy's edition was reprinted in 1843 and 1864, and again in 1902.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199418_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199418-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A new version of the book was produced by Henry Collins in 1877. It became still better known after the publication of <a href="/wiki/Grace_Harriet_Warrack" class="mw-redirect" title="Grace Harriet Warrack">Grace Warrack</a>'s 1901 edition, which included modernised language, as well as, according to the author Georgia Ronan Crampton, a "sympathetic informed introduction". The book introduced most early 20th century readers to Julian's writings;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199418_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199418-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> according to the historian <a href="/wiki/Henrietta_Leyser" title="Henrietta Leyser">Henrietta Leyser</a>, Julian was "beloved in the 20th century by theologians and poets alike".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002218–219_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002218–219-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julian's shorter work, now known as the <i>Short Text</i>, was probably written not long after her visions in May 1373.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xx–xxi_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xx–xxi-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> As with the <i>Long Text</i>, the original manuscript was lost, but not before at least one copy was made by a scribe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201674–75_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201674–75-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> It was in the possession of an English Catholic family at one point.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeechWard199512_52-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeechWard199512-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The copy was seen and described by the <a href="/wiki/Antiquarian" title="Antiquarian">antiquarian</a> <a href="/wiki/Francis_Blomefield" title="Francis Blomefield">Francis Blomefield</a> in 1745.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf20139_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf20139-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlomefieldParkin180581_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomefieldParkin180581-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> After disappearing from view for 150 years, it was found in 1910, in a collection of contemplative medieval texts bought by the <a href="/wiki/British_Museum" title="British Museum">British Museum</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf20136_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf20136-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> and was published by the Reverend Dundas Harford in 1911.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf20139_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf20139-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Now part of British Library Add MS 37790, the manuscript—with Julian's <i>Short Text</i> of 33 pages (<a href="/wiki/Folios" class="mw-redirect" title="Folios">folios</a> 97<a href="/wiki/Recto_and_verso" title="Recto and verso">r</a> to 115r)—is held in the British Library.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015li–lii_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015li–lii-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BL37790_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BL37790-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:40em; ;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>From the time these things were first revealed I had often wanted to know what was our Lord's meaning. It was more than fifteen years after that I was answered in my spirit's understanding. "You would know our Lord's meaning in this thing? Know it well. Love was His meaning. Who showed it to you? Love. What did He show you? Love. Why did He show it? For love. Hold on to this and you will know and understand love more and more. But you will not know or learn anything else&#160;&#8211;&#32;ever."
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="right-aligned" style="">Julian of Norwich,&#32;<i>Revelations of Divine Love</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002220_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002220-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>Julian of Norwich is now recognised as one of England's most important mystics;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPelphrey198914_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPelphrey198914-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> according to Leyser, she was the greatest English anchoress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeyser2002218_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeyser2002218-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> For the theologian <a href="/wiki/Denys_Turner" title="Denys Turner">Denys Turner</a> the core issue Julian addresses in <i>Revelations of Divine Love</i> is "the problem of sin". Julian says that sin is <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/behovely" class="extiw" title="wikt:behovely"><i>behovely</i></a>, which is often translated as "necessary", "appropriate", or "fitting".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatsonJenkins2006208_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatsonJenkins2006208-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETurner201152_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner201152-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julian lived in a time of turmoil, but her theology was optimistic and spoke of God's <a href="/wiki/Omnibenevolence" title="Omnibenevolence">omnibenevolence</a> and love in terms of joy and <a href="/wiki/Compassion" title="Compassion">compassion</a>. <i>Revelations of Divine Love</i> "contains a message of optimism based on the certainty of being loved by God and of being protected by his Providence".<sup id="cite_ref-Benedict_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benedict-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A characteristic element of Julian's mystical theology was her equating divine love with motherly love, a theme found in the Biblical prophets, as in <a href="/wiki/Isaiah_49" title="Isaiah 49">Isaiah 49</a>:15.<sup id="cite_ref-Benedict_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benedict-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> According to Julian, God is both our mother and our father. As the <a href="/wiki/Medieval_studies" title="Medieval studies">medievalist</a> <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Walker_Bynum" title="Caroline Walker Bynum">Caroline Walker Bynum</a> shows, this idea was also developed by <a href="/wiki/Bernard_of_Clairvaux" title="Bernard of Clairvaux">Bernard of Clairvaux</a> and others from the 12th<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>century onward.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBynum1984111–112_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBynum1984111–112-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Bynum regards the medieval notion of Jesus as a mother as being a <a href="/wiki/Metaphor" title="Metaphor">metaphor</a> rather than a literal belief.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBynum1984130_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBynum1984130-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In her fourteenth revelation, Julian writes of the <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">Trinity</a> in domestic terms, comparing Jesus to a mother who is wise, loving and merciful. Author Frances Beer asserted that Julian believed that the maternal aspect of Christ was literal and not metaphoric: Christ is not like a mother, he is literally the mother.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer1992152_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer1992152-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Julian emphasised this by explaining how the bond between mother and child is the only earthly relationship that comes close to the relationship a person can have with Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer1992155_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer1992155-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> She used metaphors when writing about Jesus in relation to ideas about conceiving, giving birth, weaning and upbringing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTED-Vasilescu201813_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTED-Vasilescu201813-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julian wrote, "For I saw no wrath except on man's side, and He forgives that in us, for wrath is nothing else but a perversity and an opposition to peace and to love."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer199845_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer199845-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> She wrote that God sees us as perfect and waits for the day when human souls mature so that evil and sin will no longer hinder us,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeer199850_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeer199850-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> and that "God is nearer to us than our own soul". This theme is repeated throughout her work: "Jesus answered with these words, saying: 'All shall be well, and all shall be well, and all manner of thing shall be well.' ... This was said so tenderly, without blame of any kind toward me or anybody else."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkinner199754–55,_124_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkinner199754–55,_124-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Her status as an anchoress may have prevented contemporary monastic and university authorities from challenging her theology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez20168–9_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez20168–9-81">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> A lack of references to her work during her own time may indicate that she kept her writings with her in her cell, so that religious authorities were unaware of them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201632_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201632-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The 14th-century English cardinal <a href="/wiki/Adam_Easton" title="Adam Easton">Adam Easton</a>'s <i>Defensorium sanctae birgittae</i>, Alfonso of Jaen's <i>Epistola Solitarii</i>, and the English mystic <a href="/wiki/William_Flete" title="William Flete">William Flete</a>'s <i>Remedies against Temptations</i>, are all referenced in Julian's text.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolloway201697–146_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolloway201697–146-83">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Commemoration">Commemoration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Commemoration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle data-file-height="1549" /></a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:141px;max-width:141px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:188px;overflow:hidden"><a 2x" data-file-width="474" data-file-height="640" /></a></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption">Depictions of Julian of Norwich (clockwise, from top left): the <a href="/wiki/Rood_screen" title="Rood screen">rood screen</a> at St Andrew and St Mary Church, <a href="/wiki/Langham,_Norfolk" title="Langham, Norfolk">Langham, Norfolk</a>; as part of the Bauchon Window, <a href="/wiki/Norwich_Cathedral" title="Norwich Cathedral">Norwich Cathedral</a>; Norwich Cathedral; St Julian's Church, Norwich; Church of St Andrew the Apostle, <a href="/wiki/Holt,_Norfolk" title="Holt, Norfolk">Holt, Norfolk</a>.</div></div></div></div>
<p>Julian is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/May_8" title="May 8">8 May</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States)" title="Episcopal Church (United States)">Episcopal Church</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_in_America" title="Evangelical Lutheran Church in America">Evangelical Lutheran Church</a> in the United States also commemorate her on 8 May.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although not canonised in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> (as of 2021<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>) or listed in the <i><a href="/wiki/Roman_Martyrology" title="Roman Martyrology">Roman Martyrology</a></i>, Julian is said to have a <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast day</a> on 13 May,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf201854_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf201854-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> and she is quoted in the <i><a href="/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church" title="Catechism of the Catholic Church">Catechism of the Catholic Church</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In 1997, Father Giandomenico Mucci listed Julian among eighteen saints and blesseds who are considered potential <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctors of the Church</a>, describing her as a <i><a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beata</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-EWTN_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EWTN-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Pope Benedict XVI">Pope Benedict XVI</a> discussed the life and teaching of Julian at a <a href="/wiki/Audience_(meeting)" title="Audience (meeting)">General Audience</a> on 1 December 2010, without describing her as either a saint or a blessed as he regularly did when discussing such persons in the same series of audiences. He stated: "Julian of Norwich understood the central message for spiritual life: God is love and it is only if one opens oneself to this love, totally and with total trust, and lets it become one's sole guide in life, that all things are transfigured, true peace and true joy found and one is able to radiate it." He concluded: "'And all will be well,' 'all manner of things shall be well': this is the final message that Julian of Norwich transmits to us and that I am also proposing to you today."<sup id="cite_ref-Benedict_71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benedict-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The 20th- and 21st-century revival of interest in Julian has been associated with a renewed interest in <a href="/wiki/Christian_contemplation" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian contemplation">Christian contemplation</a> in the English-speaking world. <a href="/wiki/The_Julian_Meetings" title="The Julian Meetings">The Julian Meetings</a>, an association of contemplative prayer groups, takes its name from her, but is unaffiliated to any faith doctrine, and is unconnected with Julian's theology, although her writings are sometimes used in meetings.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="St_Julian.27s_Church"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="St_Julian's_Church">St Julian's Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: St Julian&#039;s Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>There were no hermits or anchorites in Norwich from 1312 until the emergence of Julian in the 1370s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrampton199411_16-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrampton199411-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> St Julian's Church, located off King Street in the south of Norwich city centre, holds regular <a href="/wiki/Church_service" title="Church service">services</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The building, which has a <a href="/wiki/Round-tower_church" title="Round-tower church">round tower</a>, is one of the 31 parish churches from a total of 58 that once existed in Norwich during the <a href="/wiki/England_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="England in the Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> of which 36 had an anchorite cell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201812_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201812-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Entrance to Mother Julian&#39;s cell" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg/220px-Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg/330px-Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg/440px-Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Entrance_to_Mother_Julian%27s_cell.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The entrance to the modern cell</div></div></div>
<p>The cell did not remain empty after Julian's death. In 1428 Julian(a) Lampett (or Lampit) moved in when <a href="/wiki/Edith_Wilton" title="Edith Wilton">Edith Wilton</a> was the prioress responsible for the church,<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> and remained in the cell until 1478 when <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Pygot" title="Margaret Pygot">Margaret Pygot</a> was prioress.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> The cell continued to be used by anchorites until the dissolution of the monasteries in the 1530s, when it was demolished and the church stripped of its <a href="/wiki/Rood_screen" title="Rood screen">rood screen</a> and statues. No <a href="/wiki/Rector_(ecclesiastical)" title="Rector (ecclesiastical)">rector</a> was appointed from then until 1581.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201815_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201815-97">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1845 St Julian's was in a poor state of repair and the east wall collapsed that year. After an appeal for funds, the church was <a href="/wiki/Victorian_restoration" title="Victorian restoration">restored</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201817–18_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201817–18-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;note 5&#93;</a></sup> The church underwent further restoration during the first half of the 20th century,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201827_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201827-101">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> but was destroyed during the <a href="/wiki/Norwich_Blitz" title="Norwich Blitz">Norwich Blitz</a> of June 1942 when the tower received a direct hit. After the war, the church was rebuilt. It now appears largely as it was before its destruction, although its tower is much reduced in height and a chapel has been built in place of the long-lost anchorite cell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201828–30_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201828–30-102">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <i>Catechism of the Catholic Church</i> quotes from <i>Revelations of Divine Love</i> in its explanation of how God can draw a greater good, even from evil.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The poet <a href="/wiki/T._S._Eliot" title="T. S. Eliot">T. S. Eliot</a> incorporated "All shall be well, and all shall be well, and all manner of thing shall be well" three times into his poem "<a href="/wiki/Little_Gidding_(poem)" title="Little Gidding (poem)">Little Gidding</a>", the fourth of his <i><a href="/wiki/Four_Quartets" title="Four Quartets">Four Quartets</a></i> (1943), as well as Julian's "the ground of our beseeching".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman2011427_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman2011427-104">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The poem renewed the English-speaking public's awareness of Julian's texts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELeechWard19951_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeechWard19951-105">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;note 6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>And all shall be well and<br />
All manner of thing shall be well<br />
When the tongues of flames are in-folded<br />
Into the crowned knot of fire<br />
And the fire and the rose are one.
</p>
</div><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>T.S. Eliot, <i>Little Gidding</i>, <i>Four Quartets</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEliot194444_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliot194444-108">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Sydney_Carter" title="Sydney Carter">Sydney Carter</a>'s song "All Shall Be Well" (sometimes called "The Bells of Norwich"), which uses words by Julian, was published in 1982.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Julian's writings have been translated into numerous languages.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERolf2018ix_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERolf2018ix-110">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Norfolk_and_Norwich">Norfolk and Norwich</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Norfolk and Norwich">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 2013 the <a href="/wiki/University_of_East_Anglia" title="University of East Anglia">University of East Anglia</a> honoured Julian by naming its new study centre the Julian Study Centre.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Norwich's first Julian Week was held in May 2013. The celebration included concerts, talks, and free events held throughout the city, with the stated aim of encouraging people "to learn about Julian and her artistic, historical and theological significance".<sup id="cite_ref-JWeek_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JWeek-112">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Lady Julian Bridge, crossing the <a href="/wiki/River_Wensum" title="River Wensum">River Wensum</a> and linking King Street and the Riverside Walk close to <a href="/wiki/Norwich_railway_station" title="Norwich railway station">Norwich railway station</a>, was named in honour of the anchoress. An example of a <a href="/wiki/Swing_bridge" title="Swing bridge">swing bridge</a>, built to allow larger vessels to approach a <a href="/wiki/Canal_basin" title="Canal basin">basin</a> further upstream, it was designed by the <a href="/wiki/Mott_MacDonald" title="Mott MacDonald">Mott MacDonald</a> Group and completed in 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Self-isolation_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic">Self-isolation during the COVID-19 pandemic</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Self-isolation during the COVID-19 pandemic">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In March 2020, during the height of the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</a>, the experiences of Julian and other anchorites were cited in advice for people around the world newly discovering <a href="/wiki/Isolation_(health_care)" title="Isolation (health care)">self-isolation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Ramirez was quoted by <a href="/wiki/BBC_News" title="BBC News">BBC News</a>, saying that "Julian was living in the wake of the Black Death, and around her repeated plagues were re-decimating an already depleted population. I think she was self-isolating. The other anchorites would have understood that by removing themselves from life this would not only give them a chance of preserving their own life but also of finding calm and quiet and focus in a chaotic world."<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works:_Revelations_of_Divine_Love">Works: <i>Revelations of Divine Love</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Works: Revelations of Divine Love">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Manuscripts">Manuscripts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Manuscripts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Long_Text"><i>Long Text</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Long Text">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><cite class="citation archive">Julian of Norwich. "<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archivesetmanuscrits.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cc103201f">MS Fonds Anglais 40 (previously Regius 8297): Liber Revelacionum Julyane, anachorite norwyche, divisé en quatre-vingt-six chapitres</a>". Anglais 40. Bibliothèque nationale de France.</cite></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite class="citation archive">Julian of Norwich. "<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://searcharchives.bl.uk/IAMS_VU2:IAMS040-002114866">Sloane MS 2499: Juliana, Mother, Anchorite of Norwich: Revelations to, of Divine Love, 1373</a>". Sloane Manuscripts. British Library.</cite></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite class="citation archive">Julian of Norwich. "<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://searcharchives.bl.uk/IAMS_VU2:IAMS040-002116095">Sloane MS 3705: Visions: Revelations to Mother Juliana in the year 1373 of the love of God in Jesus Christ</a>". Sloane Manuscripts. British Library.</cite></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Westminster_Cathedral" title="Westminster Cathedral">Westminster Cathedral</a> Treasury MS 4 (W), a late 15th or early 16th century manuscript. It includes extracts from Julian's <i>Long Text</i>, as well as selections from the writings of the English mystic <a href="/wiki/Walter_Hilton" title="Walter Hilton">Walter Hilton</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECré20111_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECré20111-116">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> The manuscript is on loan to Westminster Abbey's Muniments Room and Library (as of 1997<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKempster1997178_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKempster1997178-117">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

<ul><li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite class="citation archive">"<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://searcharchives.bl.uk/IAMS_VU2:IAMS040-002054226">Add MS 37790: A Carthusian anthology of theological works in English (the 'Amherst Manuscript')</a>". Belonged to William Amhurst Tyssen-Amherst, Baron Amherst of Hackney. British Library. Retrieved 12 October 2021.</cite></li></ul>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Julian_of_Norwich&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-4">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Sources do not all agree on the year that Julian of Norwich was born; Windeatt gives late 1342; Ramirez states she was born "around 1343".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWindeatt2015xiv-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez20165_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez20165-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-7"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-7">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">A lack of data about Norwich's population during this period in its history means that it is not known for certain that the city ranked as second in size after London, although Norwich was recorded as having 130 individual trades at the end of the 13th century, in comparison with 175 for London, and more than any other regional centre in England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERawcliffeWilson2004158_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERawcliffeWilson2004158-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-15"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-15">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">It has been assumed by the historian <a href="/wiki/Janina_Ramirez" title="Janina Ramirez">Janina Ramirez</a> that Sarah was Julian's maid, and her link to the outside world. According to Ramirez, she probably had access to Julian by means of a smaller adjoining room.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamirez201618_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamirez201618-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-42"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-42">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Apart from <i>The Ancrene Riwle</i> and <i>De institutione inclusarum</i>, the most important of the 13 surviving texts are <a href="/wiki/Richard_Rolle" title="Richard Rolle">Richard Rolle</a>'s <i>Form of Living</i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1348</span>) and <i>The Scale of Perfection</i> (written by Walter Hilton in 1386 and later, prior to his death in 1396).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1993148-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-100"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-100">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to the author Sheila Upjohn and the church historian Nicholas Groves, "The restoration of the church, when [the rector] was finally forced to take action after half a century of neglect, was ruthless to the point of vandalism."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201818_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUpjohnGroves201818-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-107"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-107">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The medievalist <a href="/wiki/Barbara_Newman" title="Barbara Newman">Barbara Newman</a> notes that Julian's saying within <i>Little Gidding</i> serves "as a refrain, much as it does in Julian's own <i>Revelations of Love</i>", and that it was included at a late stage in the poem's development, after it had been worked on by Eliot for more than a year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman2011427–428_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman2011427–428-106">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
